</div>
</div>
<p>Up to 1990, English, <a href="/wiki/German_language_in_Namibia" title="German language in Namibia">German</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Afrikaans" title="Afrikaans">Afrikaans</a> were official languages. Long before Namibia's independence from South Africa, SWAPO was of the opinion that the country should become officially monolingual, choosing this approach in contrast to that of its neighbour South Africa (which granted <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_South_Africa" title="Languages of South Africa">all 11 of its major languages</a> official status), which it saw as "a deliberate policy of ethnolinguistic fragmentation."<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> Consequently, SWAPO instituted English as Namibia's sole official language, though only about 3% of the population speaks it as a home language. Its implementation is focused on the civil service, education and the broadcasting system, especially the state broadcaster NBC.<sup id="cite_ref-kriger_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kriger-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Some other languages have received semi-official recognition by being allowed as medium of instruction in primary schools. Private schools are expected to follow the same policy as state schools, and "English language" is a compulsory subject.<sup id="cite_ref-kriger_172-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kriger-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Some critics argue that, as in other postcolonial African societies, the push for monolingual instruction and policy has resulted in a high rate of school drop-outs and of individuals whose academic competence in any language is low.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the 2011 census, the most common languages are <a href="/wiki/Ovambo_language" title="Ovambo language">Oshiwambo</a> (the most spoken language for 49% of households),<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Khoekhoe_language" title="Khoekhoe language">Khoekhoegowab</a>  (11.3%), Afrikaans (10.4%), <a href="/wiki/Kwangali_language" title="Kwangali language">RuKwangali</a> (9%), and <a href="/wiki/Otjiherero" class="mw-redirect" title="Otjiherero">Otjiherero</a> (9%).<sup id="cite_ref-snapshot_164-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-snapshot-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-census2011_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-census2011-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> The most widely understood national language is <a href="/wiki/Afrikaans_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Afrikaans language">Afrikaans</a>, the country's lingua franca. Both Afrikaans and English are used primarily as a second language reserved for public communication. A complete list of languages according to the 2011 census is 48.9% <a href="/wiki/Ovambo_language" title="Ovambo language">Oshiwambo</a>, 11.3% <a href="/wiki/Khoekhoe_language" title="Khoekhoe language">Khoekhoegowab</a>, 10.4% <a href="/wiki/Afrikaans_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Afrikaans language">Afrikaans</a>, 8.6% <a href="/wiki/Otjiherero_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Otjiherero language">Otjiherero</a>, 8.5% <a href="/wiki/Kwangali_language" title="Kwangali language">RuKwangali</a>, 4.8% <a href="/wiki/Lozi_language" title="Lozi language">siLozi</a>, 3.4% <a href="/wiki/English_language" title="English language">English</a>, 1.2% Other <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Africa" title="Languages of Africa">African languages</a>, 0.9% <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German</a>, 0.8% San, 0.7% Other <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Europe" title="Languages of Europe">European languages</a>, 0.3% <a href="/wiki/Setswana_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Setswana language">Setswana</a>, and 0.1% <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Asia" title="Languages of Asia">Asian languages</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-nsa_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nsa-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Most of the white population speaks either Afrikaans or German. More than a century after the end of the German colonial era, German continues to play a role as a commercial language. Afrikaans is spoken by 60% of the white community, German by 32%, English by 7% and Portuguese by 4–5%.<sup id="cite_ref-CIA_158-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Geographical proximity to Portuguese-speaking <a href="/wiki/Angola" title="Angola">Angola</a> explains the relatively high number of <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_speaker" class="mw-redirect" title="Portuguese speaker">Portuguese speakers</a>; in 2011 these were estimated to number 100,000.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Namibia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Health">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Health_in_Namibia" title="Health in Namibia">Health in Namibia</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_Namibia" title="HIV/AIDS in Namibia">HIV/AIDS in Namibia</a></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Life_expectancy" title="Life expectancy">Life expectancy</a> at birth is estimated to be 64 years in 2017&#160;– among the lowest in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Namibia launched a National Health Extension Programme in 2012<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> deployment 1,800 (2015) of a total ceiling of 4,800 health extension workers trained for six months in community health activities including first aid, health promotion for disease prevention, nutritional assessment and counseling, water sanitation and hygiene practices, HIV testing and community-based antiretroviral treatment.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Namibia faces a non-communicable disease burden. The Demographic and Health Survey (2013) summarises findings on elevated blood pressure, hypertension, diabetes, and obesity:
</p>
<ul><li>Among eligible respondents age 35–64, more than 4 in 10 women (44 percent) and men (45 percent) have elevated blood pressure or are currently taking medicine to lower their blood pressure.</li>
<li>Forty-nine percent of women and 61 percent of men are not aware that they have elevated blood pressure.</li>
<li>Forty-three percent of women and 34 percent of men with hypertension are taking medication for their condition.</li>
<li>Only 29 percent of women and 20 percent of men with hypertension are taking medication and have their blood pressure under control.</li>
<li>Six percent of women and 7 percent of men are diabetic; that is, they have elevated fasting plasma glucose values or report that they are taking diabetes medication. An additional 7 percent of women and 6 percent of men are prediabetic.</li>
<li>Sixty-seven percent of women and 74 percent of men with diabetes are taking medication to lower their blood glucose.</li>
<li>Women and men with a higher-than-normal body mass index (25.0 or higher) are more likely to have elevated blood pressure and elevated fasting blood glucose.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:292px;"><a href="/wiki/File:AIDS_and_HIV_prevalence_2008.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/AIDS_and_HIV_prevalence_2008.svg/290px-AIDS_and_HIV_prevalence_2008.svg.png" decoding="async" width="290" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/AIDS_and_HIV_prevalence_2008.svg/435px-AIDS_and_HIV_prevalence_2008.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/AIDS_and_HIV_prevalence_2008.svg/580px-AIDS_and_HIV_prevalence_2008.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="940" data-file-height="477" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:AIDS_and_HIV_prevalence_2008.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Estimated percentage of HIV among young adults (15–49) per country as of 2011<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Namibia&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> <div> <table class="multicol" role="presentation" style="border-collapse: collapse; padding: 0; border: 0; background:transparent; width:100%;">  <tbody><tr> <td style="text-align: left; vertical-align: top;"> <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r981673959">.mw-parser-output .legend{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .legend-color{display:inline-block;min-width:1.25em;height:1.25em;line-height:1.25;margin:1px 0;text-align:center;border:1px solid black;background-color:transparent;color:black}.mw-parser-output .legend-text{}</style><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#b00000; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160;<small>15–50</small></div> &#32; </td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_Namibia" title="HIV/AIDS in Namibia">HIV epidemic</a> remains a public health issue in Namibia despite significant achievements made by the Ministry of Health and Social Services to expand HIV treatment services.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> In 2001, there were an estimated 210,000 people living with HIV/AIDS, and the estimated death toll in 2003 was 16,000. According to the 2011 UNAIDS Report, the epidemic in Namibia "appears to be leveling off."<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> As the HIV/AIDS epidemic has reduced the working-aged population, the number of orphans has increased. It falls to the government to provide education, food, shelter and clothing for these orphans.<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> A Demographic and Health Survey with an HIV biomarker was completed in <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://dhsprogram.com/pubs/pdf/FR298/FR298.pdf">2013</a> and served as the fourth comprehensive, national-level population and health survey conducted in Namibia as part of the global Demographic and Health Surveys (DHS) programme. The DHS observed important characteristics associated to the HIV epidemic:
</p>
<ul><li>Overall, 26 percent of men age 15–49 and 32 percent of those age 50–64 have been circumcised.&#160;HIV prevalence for men age 15–49 is lower among circumcised (8.0 percent) than among uncircumcised men (11.9 percent). The pattern of lower HIV prevalence among circumcised than uncircumcised men is observed across most background characteristics. For each age group, circumcised men have lower HIV prevalence than those who are not circumcised; the difference is especially pronounced for men age 35–39 and 45–49 (11.7 percentage points each). The difference in HIV prevalence between uncircumcised and circumcised men is larger among urban than rural men (5.2 percentage points versus 2.1 percentage points).</li>
<li>HIV prevalence among respondents age 15–49 is 16.9 percent for women and 10.9 percent for men. HIV prevalence rates among women and men age 50–64 are similar (16.7 percent and 16.0 percent, respectively).</li>
<li>HIV prevalence peaks in the 35–39 age group for both women and men (30.9 percent and 22.6 percent, respectively). It is lowest among respondents age 15–24 (2.5–6.4 percent for women and 2.0–3.4 percent for men).</li>
<li>Among respondents age 15–49, HIV prevalence is highest for women and men in Zambezi (30.9 percent and 15.9 percent, respectively) and lowest for women in Omaheke (6.9 percent) and men in Ohangwena (6.6 percent).</li>
<li>In 76.4 percent of the 1,007 cohabiting couples who were tested for HIV in the 2013 NDHS, both partners were HIV negative; in 10.1 percent of the couples, both partners were HIV positive; and 13.5 percent of the couples were discordant (that is, one partner was infected with HIV and the other was not).<sup id="cite_ref-:3_181-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>As of 2015, the Ministry of Health and Social Services and UNAIDS produced a Progress <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.unaids.org/sites/default/files/country/documents/NAM_narrative_report_2015.pdf">Report</a> in which <a href="/wiki/UNAIDS" class="mw-redirect" title="UNAIDS">UNAIDS</a> projected HIV prevalence among 15–49-year-olds at 13.3% [12.2–14.5%] and an estimated 210,000 [200,000–230,000] living with HIV.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Malaria" title="Malaria">malaria</a> problem seems to be compounded by the AIDS epidemic. Research has shown that in Namibia the risk of contracting malaria is 14.5% greater if a person is also infected with HIV. The risk of death from malaria is also raised by approximately 50% with a concurrent HIV infection.<sup id="cite_ref-Korenromp_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Korenromp-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> The country had only 598 physicians in 2002.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Namibia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sport">Sport</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Namibia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Sport">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Sport_in_Namibia" title="Sport in Namibia">Sport in Namibia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rugby_union_in_Namibia" title="Rugby union in Namibia">Rugby union in Namibia</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Namibia_Rugby_Team.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Namibia_Rugby_Team.jpg/170px-Namibia_Rugby_Team.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Namibia_Rugby_Team.jpg/255px-Namibia_Rugby_Team.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Namibia_Rugby_Team.jpg/340px-Namibia_Rugby_Team.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="1200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Namibia_Rugby_Team.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Namibia_national_rugby_union_team" title="Namibia national rugby union team">Namibia rugby team</a></div></div></div>
<p>The most popular sport in Namibia is <a href="/wiki/Association_football" title="Association football">association football</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Namibia_national_football_team" title="Namibia national football team">Namibia national football team</a> qualified for the <a href="/wiki/1998_Africa_Cup_of_Nations" class="mw-redirect" title="1998 Africa Cup of Nations">1998</a>, <a href="/wiki/2008_Africa_Cup_of_Nations" title="2008 Africa Cup of Nations">2008</a> and <a href="/wiki/2019_Africa_Cup_of_Nations" title="2019 Africa Cup of Nations">2019</a> editions of the <a href="/wiki/Africa_Cup_of_Nations" title="Africa Cup of Nations">Africa Cup of Nations</a>, but has yet to qualify for the <a href="/wiki/FIFA_World_Cup" title="FIFA World Cup">World Cup</a>.
</p><p>The most successful national team is the <a href="/wiki/Namibia_national_rugby_union_team" title="Namibia national rugby union team">Namibian rugby team</a>, having competed in six separate World Cups. Namibia were participants in the <a href="/wiki/1999_Rugby_World_Cup" title="1999 Rugby World Cup">1999</a>, <a href="/wiki/2003_Rugby_World_Cup" title="2003 Rugby World Cup">2003</a>, <a href="/wiki/2007_Rugby_World_Cup" title="2007 Rugby World Cup">2007</a>, <a href="/wiki/2011_Rugby_World_Cup" title="2011 Rugby World Cup">2011</a>, <a href="/wiki/2015_Rugby_World_Cup" title="2015 Rugby World Cup">2015</a> and 2019 <a href="/wiki/Rugby_World_Cup" title="Rugby World Cup">Rugby World Cups</a>. <a href="/wiki/Cricket" title="Cricket">Cricket</a> is also popular, with the <a href="/wiki/Namibia_national_cricket_team" title="Namibia national cricket team">national side</a> having qualified for <a href="/wiki/2003_Cricket_World_Cup" title="2003 Cricket World Cup">2003 Cricket World Cup</a>, <a href="/wiki/2021_ICC_T20_World_Cup" class="mw-redirect" title="2021 ICC T20 World Cup">2021 ICC T20 World Cup</a> and <a href="/wiki/2022_ICC_Men%27s_T20_World_Cup" title="2022 ICC Men&#39;s T20 World Cup">2022 ICC Men's T20 World Cup</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> In December 2017, Namibia Cricket reached the final of the Cricket South Africa (CSA) Provincial One Day Challenge for the first time.<sup id="cite_ref-NCYR_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NCYR-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> In February 2018 Namibia hosted the ICC World Cricket League Division 2 with Namibia, Kenya, UAE, Nepal, Canada and Oman to compete for the final two ICC Cricket World Cup Qualifier positions in Zimbabwe. Namibia also qualified the qualifiers of ICC T20 World Cup 2021 and entered the super 12 club.<sup id="cite_ref-NCYR_190-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NCYR-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The most famous athlete from Namibia is <a href="/wiki/Frankie_Fredericks" title="Frankie Fredericks">Frankie Fredericks</a>, sprinter in the 100 and 200 m events. He won four Olympic silver medals (1992, 1996) and also has medals from several <a href="/wiki/IAAF_World_Championships_in_Athletics" class="mw-redirect" title="IAAF World Championships in Athletics">World Athletics Championships</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> Golfer <a href="/wiki/Trevor_Dodds" title="Trevor Dodds">Trevor Dodds</a> won the <a href="/wiki/Greater_Greensboro_Open" class="mw-redirect" title="Greater Greensboro Open">Greater Greensboro Open</a> in 1998, one of 15 tournaments in his career. He achieved a career-high world ranking of 78th in 1998.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Professional cyclist and Namibian Road Race champion <a href="/wiki/Dan_Craven" title="Dan Craven">Dan Craven</a> represented Namibia at the <a href="/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics" title="2016 Summer Olympics">2016 Summer Olympics</a> in both the road race and individual time trial.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> Boxer <a href="/wiki/Julius_Indongo" title="Julius Indongo">Julius Indongo</a> is the unified WBA, IBF, and IBO world champion in the <a href="/wiki/Light_welterweight" title="Light welterweight">Light welterweight</a> division. Another famous athlete from Namibia is ex-professional rugby player <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Burger" title="Jacques Burger">Jacques Burger</a>. Burger played for <a href="/wiki/Saracens_Rugby_Club" class="mw-redirect" title="Saracens Rugby Club">Saracens</a> and <a href="/wiki/Stade_Aurillacois_Cantal_Auvergne" title="Stade Aurillacois Cantal Auvergne">Aurillac</a> in Europe, as well as gaining 41 caps for the national team.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Media">Media</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Namibia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Media">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Media_of_Namibia" class="mw-redirect" title="Media of Namibia">Media of Namibia</a></div>
<p>Although Namibia's population is fairly small, the country has a diverse choice of media; two TV stations, 19 radio stations (without counting community stations), 5 daily newspapers, several weeklies and special publications compete for the attention of the audience. Additionally, a mentionable amount of foreign media, especially South African, is available. Online media are mostly based on print publication contents. Namibia has a state-owned Press Agency, called <a href="/wiki/Namibia_Press_Agency" title="Namibia Press Agency">NAMPA</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothe_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothe-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> Overall <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 300 journalists work in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-jobs_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jobs-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first newspaper in Namibia was the German-language <i>Windhoeker Anzeiger</i>, founded 1898. During German rule, the newspapers mainly reflected the living reality and the view of the white German-speaking minority. The black majority was ignored or depicted as a threat. During South African rule, the white bias continued, with mentionable influence of the Pretoria government on the South West African media system. Independent newspapers were seen as a menace to the existing order, and critical journalists were often threatened.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothe_193-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothe-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Links_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Links-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Current daily newspapers are the private publications <i><a href="/wiki/The_Namibian" title="The Namibian">The Namibian</a></i> (English and other languages), <i><a href="/wiki/Die_Republikein" class="mw-redirect" title="Die Republikein">Die Republikein</a></i> (Afrikaans), <i><a href="/wiki/Allgemeine_Zeitung_(Namibia)" title="Allgemeine Zeitung (Namibia)">Allgemeine Zeitung</a></i> (German) and <i><a href="/wiki/Namibian_Sun" title="Namibian Sun">Namibian Sun</a></i> (English) as well as the state-owned <i><a href="/wiki/New_Era_(Namibia)" title="New Era (Namibia)">New Era</a></i> (predominantly English). Except for the largest newspaper, <i>The Namibian</i>, which is owned by a trust, the other mentioned private newspapers are part of Democratic Media Holdings.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothe_193-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothe-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> Other mentionable newspapers are the tabloid <i>Informanté</i> owned by TrustCo, the weekly <i><a href="/wiki/Windhoek_Observer" title="Windhoek Observer">Windhoek Observer</a></i>, the weekly <i><a href="/wiki/Namibia_Economist" title="Namibia Economist">Namibia Economist</a></i>, as well as the regional <i>Namib Times</i>. Current affairs magazines include <i><a href="/wiki/Insight_Namibia" title="Insight Namibia">Insight Namibia</a></i>, <i>Vision2030 Focus magazine</i><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2015)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and <i>Prime FOCUS</i>. The <i><a href="/wiki/Sister_Namibia" title="Sister Namibia">Sister Namibia</a></i> magazine stands out as the longest running NGO magazine in Namibia, while <i><a href="/wiki/Namibia_Sport" title="Namibia Sport">Namibia Sport</a></i> is the only national sport magazine. Furthermore, the print market is complemented with party publications, student newspapers and PR publications.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothe_193-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothe-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Radio was introduced in 1969, TV in 1981. The broadcasting sector today is dominated by the state-run <a href="/wiki/Namibian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Namibian Broadcasting Corporation">Namibian Broadcasting Corporation</a> (NBC). The <a href="/wiki/Public_broadcasting" title="Public broadcasting">public broadcaster</a> offers a TV station as well as a "National Radio" in English and nine language services in locally spoken languages. The nine private radio stations in the country are mainly English-language channels, except for Radio Omulunga (Oshiwambo) and Kosmos 94.1 (Afrikaans).
Privately held <a href="/wiki/One_Africa_Television" title="One Africa Television">One Africa TV</a> has competed with NBC since the 2000s.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothe_193-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothe-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-oneafricabackground_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oneafricabackground-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Compared to neighbouring countries, Namibia has a large degree of media freedom. Over the past years, the country usually ranked in the upper quarter of the <a href="/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index" title="Press Freedom Index">Press Freedom Index</a> of <a href="/wiki/Reporters_without_Borders" class="mw-redirect" title="Reporters without Borders">Reporters without Borders</a>, reaching position 21 in 2010, being on par with Canada and the best-positioned African country.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> The African Media Barometer shows similarly positive results. However, as in other countries, there is still mentionable influence of representatives of state and economy on media in Namibia.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothe_193-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothe-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> In 2009, Namibia dropped to position 36 on the Press Freedom Index.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> In 2013, it was 19th,<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> 22nd in 2014<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> and 23rd in 2019,<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> meaning that it is currently the highest ranked African country in terms of press freedom.
</p><p>Media and journalists in Namibia are represented by the Namibian chapter of the <a href="/wiki/Media_Institute_of_Southern_Africa" title="Media Institute of Southern Africa">Media Institute of Southern Africa</a> and the Editors' Forum of Namibia. An independent media ombudsman was appointed in 2009 to prevent a state-controlled media council.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothe_193-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothe-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Namibia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:42817_06.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/42817_06.JPG/220px-42817_06.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="149" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/42817_06.JPG/330px-42817_06.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/42817_06.JPG/440px-42817_06.JPG 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="434" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:42817_06.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Secondary school students</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Namibia" title="Education in Namibia">Education in Namibia</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_schools_in_Namibia" title="List of schools in Namibia">List of schools in Namibia</a></div>
<p>Namibia has free education for both primary and secondary education levels. Grades 1–7 are primary level, grades 8–12 are secondary. In 1998, there were 400,325 Namibian students in primary school and 115,237 students in secondary schools. The pupil-teacher ratio in 1999 was estimated at 32:1, with about 8% of the GDP being spent on education. Curriculum development, educational research, and professional development of teachers is centrally organised by the National Institute for Educational Development (NIED) in Okahandja.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> Among sub-Saharan African countries, Namibia has one of the highest literacy rates.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/CIA_World_Factbook" class="mw-redirect" title="CIA World Factbook">CIA World Factbook</a>, as of 2018 91.5% of the population age 15 and over can read and write.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_204-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Most schools in Namibia are state-run, but there are some private schools, which are also part of the country's education system. There are four teacher training universities, three colleges of agriculture, a police training college, and three universities: <a href="/wiki/University_of_Namibia" title="University of Namibia">University of Namibia</a> (UNAM), <a href="/wiki/International_University_of_Management" title="International University of Management">International University of Management</a> (IUM) and <a href="/wiki/Namibia_University_of_Science_and_Technology" title="Namibia University of Science and Technology">Namibia University of Science and Technology</a> (NUST). Namibia was ranked 100th in the <a href="/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index" title="Global Innovation Index">Global Innovation Index</a> in 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-Dual-10"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-Dual_10-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Dual_10-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFShugart2005" class="citation journal cs1">Shugart, Matthew Søberg (December 2005). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://link.springer.com/content/pdf/10.1057%2Fpalgrave.fp.8200087.pdf">"Semi-Presidential Systems: Dual Executive And Mixed Authority Patterns"</a> <span class="cs1-format">(PDF)</span>. <i>French Politics</i>. <b>3</b> (3): 323–351. <a href="/wiki/Doi_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="Doi (identifier)">doi</a>:<span class="cs1-lock-free" title="Freely accessible"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://doi.org/10.1057%2Fpalgrave.fp.8200087">10.1057/palgrave.fp.8200087</a></span>. <a href="/wiki/S2CID_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="S2CID (identifier)">S2CID</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID:73642272">73642272</a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">4 September</span> 2016</span>. <q>Of the contemporary cases, only four provide the assembly majority an unrestricted right to vote no confidence, and of these, only two allow the president unrestricted authority to appoint the prime minister. These two, Mozambique and Namibia, as well as the Weimar Republic, thus resemble most closely the structure of authority depicted in the right panel of Figure 3, whereby the dual accountability of the cabinet to both the president and the assembly is maximized. (...) Namibia allows the president to dissolve <i>[the assembly]</i> at any time but places a novel negative incentive on his exercise of the right: He must stand for a new election at the same time as the new assembly elections.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=French+Politics&amp;rft.atitle=Semi-Presidential+Systems%3A+Dual+Executive+And+Mixed+Authority+Patterns&amp;rft.volume=3&amp;rft.issue=3&amp;rft.pages=323-351&amp;rft.date=2005-12&amp;rft_id=info%3Adoi%2F10.1057%2Fpalgrave.fp.8200087&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fapi.semanticscholar.org%2FCorpusID%3A73642272%23id-name%3DS2CID&amp;rft.aulast=Shugart&amp;rft.aufirst=Matthew+S%C3%B8berg&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Flink.springer.com%2Fcontent%2Fpdf%2F10.1057%252Fpalgrave.fp.8200087.pdf&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ANamibia" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-27"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-27">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFDavid_Olusoga2015" class="citation web cs1"><a href="/wiki/David_Olusoga" title="David Olusoga">David Olusoga</a> (18 April 2015). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.theguardian.com/commentisfree/2015/apr/18/pope-francis-armenian-genocide-first-20th-century-namibia">"Dear Pope Francis, Namibia was the 20th century's first genocide"</a>. <i>The Guardian</i><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">26 November</span> 2015</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=The+Guardian&amp;rft.atitle=Dear+Pope+Francis%2C+Namibia+was+the+20th+century%27s+first+genocide&amp;rft.date=2015-04-18&amp;rft.au=David+Olusoga&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.theguardian.com%2Fcommentisfree%2F2015%2Fapr%2F18%2Fpope-francis-armenian-genocide-first-20th-century-namibia&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ANamibia" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-28"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-28">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Drechsler, Horst (1980). The actual number of deaths in the limited number of battles with the Germany Schutztruppe (expeditionary force) were limited; most of the deaths occurred after fighting had ended. The German military governor <a href="/wiki/Lothar_von_Trotha" title="Lothar von Trotha">Lothar von Trotha</a> issued an explicit extermination order, and many Herero died of disease and abuse in detention camps after being taken from their land. A substantial minority of Herero crossed the Kalahari desert into the British colony of Bechuanaland (modern-day <a href="/wiki/Botswana" title="Botswana">Botswana</a>), where a small community continues to live in western Botswana near to border with Namibia. <i>Let us die fighting</i>, originally published (1966) under the title <i>Südwestafrika unter deutscher Kolonialherrschaft</i>. Berlin: Akademie-Verlag.</span>
</li>
